| `remainingSeconds`, `remainingBasis` | An estimate of the time left: the tiles still to fetch times the seconds one takes. `remainingBasis` is `measured` from the pace the task itself reached once it had fetched enough tiles, else from the mirror's measured pace towards the CDS, and `assumed` while nothing has been measured. Waiting time is not included; tiles already on disk are counted as still to fetch, so after a resume it is an upper bound. `0` when `complete`, `null` when `idle` or `failed`. |
| `reason`                             | While `waiting`: `upstream_unavailable` (the CDS does not answer), `storage_error` (the mirror could not write) or `internal_error`. While `failed`: `upstream_rejected` (the CDS refuses a tile for good: a status other than `429`/`5xx`) or `internal_error`. `null` otherwise.                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| `nextAttemptAt`                      | The time of the next attempt (ISO 8601) while `waiting`, else `null`.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| `runningTier`                        | The tier the service is preparing right now — not necessarily `tier` when this one is `queued` — or `null`.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| `queuePosition`                      | 1-based, while `queued`, else `null`.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| `retryAfterSeconds`                  | When to read again, also sent as `Retry-After`: 15 while `running`, 30 while `queued`, 15 to 60 while `waiting` (the time to the next attempt, within those bounds); `null`, with no header, in every other state.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| `startedAt`, `serverTime`            | ISO 8601 times: when this task started running (`null` until then), and now, on the portal's clock.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |

The task builds the archives one after the other in the order a client downloads
them — the fixed low-order archive first, then order by order, pixel by pixel —
skipping any that exist. One task runs at a time for the whole portal: a request
for a tier the running one covers (tiers are cumulative: `comfort` holds every
archive `standard` does) joins it; a larger one is `queued` and starts by
itself. It goes through the mirror's own connection limit and host breakers,
never faster than a client would, and an archive a client asks for directly
keeps being built and served as before — `503` `sky_tiles_archive_building`
with its `code`, `done`, `total` and `Retry-After`, `Range`, `no-transform` — and
ahead of the task's own tiles.

It never loops on a failure. A CDS that does not answer puts the task in
`waiting` (`upstream_unavailable`): the first retry comes 30 seconds later, the
next 60, doubling up to five minutes, and never before the breaker on the CDS
host allows one; `nextAttemptAt` says when. A tile the CDS refuses for good ends
the task in `failed`. Tiles already fetched are never fetched again, whichever
way the task ended.

Nothing about a task is kept on disk. After a restart of the service — a
deployment, or the wake-up of a service that scales to zero — a `GET` reads what
is on disk: `archives` is right, `state` is `idle`, and the client sends the
`POST` again; the archives built so far, and the tiles of the one that was
interrupted, are found and not fetched again. A service that scales to zero goes
to sleep after some minutes without a request, and a task at work does not count
as one: a client that wants the preparation to go on reads the state at least
once a minute, and preferably every `retryAfterSeconds`; when it stops, the
service eventually sleeps and the task stops with it.

The mirror's size limit (`ASTRO_PORTAL_SKY_TILE_MIRROR_MAX_BYTES`, 4 GiB by
default) evicts the least recently used files past it. The archives of a tier
being prepared are protected from that eviction for as long as the task lives —
the tiles of archives already built are what goes first — and a tier whose
archives, plus the tiles of the one being built, could not fit under the limit
is refused before anything is fetched, rather than evicting its own archives as
it goes: `409` `sky_backdrop_tier_exceeds_mirror_limit`, with `tier`,
`requiredBytes` (an estimate from the per-order tile sizes), `limitBytes` and
`minimumLimitBytes` (the smallest limit that accepts it) at the top of the body
and the setting to raise in `error.message`. With the default limit, `quality`
(about 5.8 GB of archives) is refused, and `comfort` (about 1.2 GB) is not.

The refusal is also said beforehand, on the manifest. A tier that is not built
yet (`archives.ready` below `archives.total`) and would be refused this way
carries `unavailable: true` (additive, and only ever `true`): it is decided by
the very same rule as the `409` — one function, not two copies of its
arithmetic — against the same tile sizes and the same limit, so a tier is
announced `unavailable` exactly when preparing it is refused. It is never on a
`ready` tier (nothing is left to fit), nor in a static pack (which prepares
nothing). A client shows such a tier as not offered from the start, instead of
offering it and finding out at the request. A tier without the field is one the
service can provide, or a service that says nothing about it: the client then
learns at the request, by the `409`, as before.

```json
{
  "error": {
    "code": "sky_backdrop_tier_exceeds_mirror_limit",
    "message": "The \"quality\" tier needs about 5791625128 bytes of this portal's sky-tile mirror, whose limit is 4294967296 bytes (3865470566 usable). Raise ASTRO_PORTAL_SKY_TILE_MIRROR_MAX_BYTES to at least 6435139032 to prepare it."
  },
  "code": "sky_backdrop_tier_exceeds_mirror_limit",
  "tier": "quality",
  "requiredBytes": 5791625128,
  "limitBytes": 4294967296,
  "minimumLimitBytes": 6435139032
}
```

## Equipment

`GET /v1/equipment` returns `{ "equipment": [...] }` for the authenticated
device's own tenant, read-only and in the same item shape the web planner's
equipment endpoint returns (id, tenantId, type, name, manufacturer, model,
isBinoculars, specifications, planner, notes, createdAt, updatedAt,
archivedAt, preferenceOrder). It is a private profile read, not plan
synchronization: it updates `lastSeenAt`, not `lastSyncAt`. The space's own items are written on the web, « Mon espace » → « Matériel »,
and on the phone, « Mon matériel »,
through the routes of [The owner's equipment](#the-owners-equipment).

`POST /v1/equipment` `{ "equipment": { type, name, … } }` (the
import of an old portal's inventory):
a paired device allowed
to write adds **one new item** to its owner's space. The body, its rules and
its refusals are those of `POST /v1/manage/equipment` (one function of the
core, `createManageEquipment().create`): the same `400` `invalid_request`
with the same message, the `id` of the body never used, the common ceiling
of a JSON body (`413` `payload_too_large`), the same change of the domain
`equipment`, the same answer, `201` `{ "equipment" }` (`item` of
[`manage-equipment.schema.json`](manage-equipment.schema.json)). The
device's right to write is the one of its other writes
(`PUT /v1/favorites/{id}`, a device's `POST /v1/uploads`): its bearer,
without touching `lastSyncAt`, and the soft-downgrade gate — an account in
the read-only window of an ended subscription answers `403`
`entitlement_read_only`, as its plans and uploads do. The phone's « Mon
matériel » edits, orders and archives through the manage routes below.

Two fields are added, additively (a client reading an older
portal finds neither and keeps its bundled profiles): `defaults`, the active
rows of « Matériel par défaut » every space is offered — the item shape
without `tenantId` and `notes`, each default row with the identifier of the
common profile it was seeded from (`seestar-s50`), a connected telescope's
`planner` carrying its `group`, `integratedCamera` and `mount`, and every
default row's `planner` its `maker` and its `kind` (the « Marque » and
« Type » menus of the pickers; migration 108 wrote them on the rows) — and
`hideDefaultEquipment`, the space's option « Masquer le matériel par
défaut ». The web planner's `GET /api/equipment` answers the same three
fields. A fourth is added, additively, to
both: `defaultConfiguration`, « Ma configuration par défaut » of the space
(`null` when none is set; see [The owner's equipment](#the-owners-equipment)). The default rows are held by the platform organisation (id
`platform`), which is no one's space.

**Filters attached to equipment.** Nothing is added to the item's
shape: four optional keys live in its `planner` object (documented, with their
types, in `equipment.schema.json` and `manage-equipment.schema.json`; a client
that does not know them keeps them and never loses them, as it keeps
`family`). On a **filter**, `equipmentIds` (ids of the person's telescopes and
cameras) and `forAllEquipment` (default `true`: offered for all equipment;
`false`: only with `equipmentIds`). On a **telescope** or a **camera**,
`ownFiltersOnly` (default `false`: its own filters and the ones offered for all
equipment; `true`: its own filters only; a telescope made from a smart-telescope
profile has it `true` unless the person turned it off). On a **telescope**,
`profileId`, the id of the common profile (`seestar-s50`) it was made from,
which gives it the built-in filters of that profile as read-only rows; a
hand-typed telescope has none. The filters a plan offers are the union of its
telescope's and its camera's: for each, its attached filters (built-in ones
included) and, unless it says `ownFiltersOnly`, the filters offered for all
equipment (the platform's defaults among them); the rule is
`filtersOfferedFor` of both planners, pinned by the fixtures
`filters-offered-for` and `acquisition-filter-groups`. The fields are written
and synchronised like the rest of the equipment (no migration: `planner` is a
free object); an item that has none reads `forAllEquipment: true`,
`equipmentIds: []`, `ownFiltersOnly: false`.

**Tracking past the meridian (MERIDIAN-LIMIT-02a).** On a **mount** that can
flip (equatorial, hybrid, or a kind not said), the optional `planner` key
`meridianLimitMinutes` is a whole number of minutes, 0 to 120: how long the
mount can keep tracking past the meridian before it flips (0: it flips at the
meridian; absent: not said, nothing is cut). Never written for an alt-az
mount, a tracker or a smart telescope. A plan inherits it and may carry its
own in `mountSafety.meridianLimitMinutes` (`mountSafety.meridianCut`, default
`true`, is the plan's switch for the cut); the service stores and compares
these fields and never computes with them. No migration: `planner` and
`mountSafety` are free objects.

## Places

A **place** belongs to the space, like a framing or an item of
equipment, and is synchronized. The normative shapes are
[`place.schema.json`](place.schema.json); the shared fixtures are
`fixtures/places/` (`places.json`, one example of every body;
`places-conflict.json`, the matrix of the rule below, replayed by the
service's tests; `places-phone-writes.json`, the bodies the phone builds from
its own list, which the service's tests send to the real routes;
`places-duplicates.json`, the rule of possible duplicates the clients
compute).

**Who.** Every route answers either credential — the web session (its CSRF
token on a write) or a paired device's token — for the credential's own
space; a place of another space is `404 place_not_found`, never named.
Every write (`PUT`, `DELETE`, `PUT …/default`, `POST …/import`) passes the
soft-downgrade gate of `POST /v1/equipment`: in the read-only window of an
ended subscription it answers `403 entitlement_read_only`; the places stay
readable, and a device keeps using its own copy while its queue waits. No
capability is involved.

**The resource.** `id` is chosen by the client and never changed: a UUID
for a new place, given as soon as its point is set (`^[A-Za-z0-9._:-]{1,120}$`;
an id stored before this contract stays valid). `name` (1 to 100
characters, kept as typed), `latitude`, `longitude` are required;
`locality` (200), `elevation` (a decimal string, absent when unknown),
`timeZone` (an IANA name; an explicit `UTC` is UTC. **A
place is never written without one.** A write that gives none is completed by
the service from the position (the exact zone boundaries); a position the
service cannot read a zone for is refused, `422` `time_zone_required`, and the
client asks the person to choose it. A place stored before this rule and still
without a zone is read by every surface in the zone of its position, then
written again by the first one that meets it), `timeZoneSource` (`chosen`
when the person picked the zone themselves — no resolution, by a client or by
the service, ever replaces it; absent for a zone that was read. Dropped when
the write has no `timeZone`), `horizonMask` (36
points, one every 10°, or `null`; each point may carry `surveyedAt`, the
instant its 10° sector was last surveyed — in augmented reality, or moved by
hand in an editor — and a point without it is « à relever »: a place stored
before this field has none, nothing is migrated), `horizonStatus`, `skyEntered`, `kind`
(`private`, `viewpoint`, `campsite`, `park`, `picnic`, or absent: an absent
`kind` is read as `private`, or as the type of the `knownPlace` when there is
one; a place created from a suggested place keeps the suggestion's type),
`knownPlace` (the link to the OpenStreetMap record the place was created from,
a reference only: `source`, `id`, `type`, `linkedAt`, never the record's
details; the person can detach it, the place and its `kind` stay) and `note`
(500, never copied into a plan) are optional. `editedAt` is the instant of the person's last gesture the
service kept; `createdAt` and `updatedAt` are the service's. `usage` is
computed at each read from the plans not deleted whose place (`location.id`)
is this one: `pendingPlans` are the plans to execute (`active`), `pastPlans`
every other one. A plan keeps its own copy of the place with the same `id`
(`planner-document.schema.json`, unchanged): a night done keeps the place
and the horizon it was planned with.

**The most recent edit wins, the place as one block.** `PUT
/v1/places/{placeId}` carries the whole place and `editedAt`, the instant of
the gesture on the device. The service caps `editedAt` at its own clock + 5
minutes (a clock ahead wins for five minutes at most) and keeps the most
recent gesture: a write more recent than what it keeps is applied (`201`
when no place of that id existed, `200` otherwise, `applied: true`); an
older or **equal** one is not, and answers `200` `applied: false` with the
place the service keeps — `null` when the place stays deleted — which the
client adopts. There is no conflict error, no copy, no comparison: two
devices that edit the name and the horizon offline keep only the most
recent edit. A field the body leaves out is absent afterwards (one block);
unknown fields are ignored.

**Deletion.** `DELETE /v1/places/{placeId}?at=<instant>` (absent: the
service's clock; capped the same way) is soft. It applies when the place
was not edited after `at` (`{ deleted: true }`); a place edited after `at`
stays and is answered (`{ deleted: false, place }`). A place used by a plan
to execute is refused `409 place_in_use`, the body naming those
`pendingPlans`: the person changes their place or deletes them first; a
plan executed, partial, abandoned or archived keeps its copy and never
blocks. A deleted place answers `404` to `GET`, `DELETE` and as a default
place; an edit more recent than the deletion's instant brings it back
(`applied: true`), an older or equal one does not (`place: null`). The
tombstone is kept 90 days after the deletion's instant, then purged; an
edit older than 90 days that finds no place never creates it again
(`applied: false`, `place: null`) — a purged deletion is never undone by a
stale edit.

**The default place.** One per space: `PUT /v1/places/default` `{ placeId |
null, editedAt }`, under the same rule (`applied`), `404` when `placeId` is
not a live place of the space. `GET /v1/places` answers it beside the list
(`defaultPlaceId`, `defaultPlaceEditedAt`); a default place deleted reads
`null`, and the clients fall back on their own rule.

**Import.** `POST /v1/places/import` `{ places }` sends what a phone or a
browser kept without a space, once (at the pairing of a phone, at the first
sign-in of a browser): at most 200, each with its own `id`, each id once.
Each id the space does not hold is created with its own `editedAt`; an id
it holds — live or deleted — is never changed (`created: false`), so a
replay changes nothing. The answer is the space's places and `imported:
[{ id, created }]` in the order sent.

**On the phone**. The phone keeps the places of its
space on the device and sends them as it does its equipment: every gesture
(a place saved, deleted, marked the default place, its note written) shows at
once and is queued; the queue is sent in order, right after the write, at each
synchronization and at the return of the network, and stops at the first write
the service does not answer (a `403` `entitlement_read_only` leaves the queue
waiting, the places stay on the phone). The service's answer is adopted: the
place it keeps replaces the phone's when the phone has not been edited since
(the zone it completed, or the more recent edit of the web, which the status
line of the phone names). The `places` domain of the change signal reads the
space again; a place the space held and no longer holds leaves the phone, a
place the space never acknowledged stays. A phone without a space keeps its
places on the device and sends them once, at the first link, by the import
(its default place and notes with them). A plan keeps its own copy of the
place: a place deleted from the list is never deleted from a plan.

**Limits and signal.** At most 200 live places per space: a creation, an
import or a place brought back beyond it is refused `409
place_limit_reached` (`limit: 200`), the import whole; an edit of a place
already there never is. Every applied write publishes the `places` domain of
the [change signal](#reactive-change-signal), after its transaction; a write
not applied publishes nothing.

**Static segments.** `known`, `search`, `suggested`, `resolve`, `extract`,
`default` and `import` are segments of other routes, resolved before
`{placeId}`: they are refused as a place's id (`400`).

**What changed for the web planner.** `PUT /api/plans` no longer writes
the places of a space nor deletes those missing from its document, and `GET
/api/plans` no longer answers `knownLocations`: the document's
`knownLocations` is only the visitor's store in the browser. A signed-in
planner reads and writes its places here.

| Code                  | Status | Meaning                                                                |
| --------------------- | ------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `place_not_found`     | `404`  | No live place of this id in the space.                                 |
| `place_in_use`        | `409`  | `DELETE` of a place a plan to execute uses; `pendingPlans` names them. |
| `place_limit_reached` | `409`  | The space would hold more than 200 places; `limit` is 200.             |

## Weather, sky quality and place lookup

`GET /v1/weather`, `GET /v1/sky-quality`, `GET /v1/places/resolve` and
`GET /v1/places/search` are public, unauthenticated-by-default reads: thin
wrappers over the same server-side functions the web planner's
`planner-weather`, `planner-sky-quality` and `planner-location` routes call,
so their cache windows, provider fallback, stale/cooldown behavior and error
codes are identical to the web planner's — the mobile app never calls a
weather, sky-quality or geocoding provider directly. As with the target
catalogue, a supplied `Authorization` header is still an intentional device
attempt: an invalid, revoked, or non-device bearer answers `401`
`device_authentication_required` rather than being ignored. None of the four
updates `lastSeenAt` or `lastSyncAt` for an anonymous caller; a valid device
bearer updates `lastSeenAt` only.

`GET /v1/weather` and `GET /v1/sky-quality` take `latitude` and `longitude`
query parameters, exactly as the web routes do. Invalid or out-of-range
coordinates answer `400` `invalid_request`. A provider outage or rate limit
answers `503` with the provider-specific unavailable code
(`weather_unavailable` or `sky_quality_unavailable`) and a `Retry-After`
header; weather additionally falls back from Open-Meteo to MET Norway before
failing, and either endpoint may serve a stale cached estimate instead of
failing outright. `GET /v1/weather` caches `private, max-age=30`; `GET
/v1/sky-quality` caches `private, max-age=86400`. Neither ever echoes the
queried coordinates back beyond what the shared cache key requires.

`GET /v1/weather`'s hourly series run from two days before today to
fifteen days after it: sixteen days of forecast, the first eleven (0 to 10
days out) reliable, the last five only a trend (`forecastDateAvailability`
answers `trend` for them); MET Norway, the fallback, answers what it can and
the days it cannot are `beyond-range`.

`GET /v1/weather/history` (schema
[`weather-history.schema.json`](weather-history.schema.json)) takes
`latitude`, `longitude` and `month` (`YYYY-MM`) and answers the measured
weather of that civil month plus one day on each side (a night spans two
civil days), never past today, from the Open-Meteo archive: the hourly
`cloud_cover` (and its three layers), `precipitation` (the amount that fell,
mm), `wind_speed_10m`, `wind_direction_10m`, `wind_gusts_10m`,
`relative_humidity_2m`, `temperature_2m`, `dew_point_2m`, and the daily
`weather_code`, with `measured: true`. The archive has neither a rain
probability nor a visibility: the night's score (`summarizeAstronomyForecast`
with `measured: true`) counts an hour with at least 0.1 mm as a certain rain
and a dry hour as none, and leaves the visibility neutral. The hours the
archive does not hold yet are read from the forecast's recent days. One
entry per rounded place and month, shared by every caller; a month whose
days are all settled stays fresh for a month, the current one for three
hours; the stale, cooldown and `503` rules are `GET /v1/weather`'s, with
`private, max-age=300`. The web planner reads the same cache through
`planner-weather-history`.

`GET /v1/places/climate-trend` (schema
[`place-climate-trend.schema.json`](place-climate-trend.schema.json)) takes
`latitude` and `longitude` and answers the weather trend of the place over the
ten calendar years finished before today, from the Open-Meteo archive: for each
civil month the night hours (Sun under -12 degrees, on the place's local solar
time, so no time zone is needed), the clear ones among them (cloud cover of at
most 30 % and under 0.1 mm of rain), their share, and the nights and the good
nights (four clear night hours); then the best period (the window of two to
four consecutive months, the year being circular, with the highest clear share)
and the worst one (the lowest that does not overlap it). A gap under ten points
between the two is no trend (`trend: "flat"`, `worst: null`); fewer than five
years with data say nothing (`trend: "insufficient"`). A paid addition of
« Planification avancée »: a device token or a web
session is required (`401`), and the profile must hold `planner.mosaic`, else
`403` `capability_required` with `capability: "planner.mosaic"` — a private
server, whose open-source profile has no `planner.mosaic`, refuses too, on
purpose; the conditions of a place stay free. The service reads the archive
once for the ten years of a place (coordinates rounded to 0.05 degree, one
request of about 1.7 MB, 300 KB compressed), keeps the trend in its database for
a year and serves it `stale: true` rather than failing once it is older or the
archive is down; a first reading that takes more than a few seconds answers `202`
`{ "status": "computing" }` with a `Retry-After` while it goes on, and a failure
with nothing kept is `503` `weather_unavailable` with `Retry-After`, as the
forecast. `private, max-age=86400`, `private, max-age=300` when stale. The web
planner reads the same cache, with the same right, through
`planner-place-climate-trend`.

`GET /v1/sky-quality` has no outbound provider at all: it samples a
self-hosted VIIRS Nighttime Lights raster prepared offline by
`packages/portal/scripts/prepare-sky-quality-raster.mjs` (see `docs/SKY_QUALITY_RASTER.md`).
Each cell of that raster holds the upward radiance of everything within
300 km of it, summed with a distance kernel at preparation time, so the
answer for a dark site near a city carries that city's glow; `method` says
so (`viirs-propagated-radiance-zenith-sky-brightness`). An installation with
no raster answers the same `503` `sky_quality_unavailable` as any other
unavailable read, and `source` names the annual composite the installed
raster was built from — `viirs-vnl-<year>`, not a fixed provider name.

The radiance-to-brightness conversion is one global constant everywhere
except where a region in `skyQualityCalibrationRegions`
(`packages/portal/src/sky-quality.js`) says otherwise — Japan, as of this
writing. The result carries an ADDITIVE, OPTIONAL `calibration` field for
that: `<calibration-set version>/<region id>`, e.g. `2026-09/japan` or
`2026-09/global`. Nothing else in the response changes when a region
applies, `method` included. A client MAY compare a cached estimate's
`calibration` against the current one and treat a mismatch as stale; none of
the four endpoints described in this document depends on that comparison
itself.

### Sky brightness by direction

`GET /v1/sky-quality/direction` takes the same
`latitude` and `longitude` and answers the estimated sky brightness, in
mag/arcsec², by altitude and azimuth around the place: the grid of
`skyBrightnessByDirection` (`packages/portal/src/sky-quality-direction.js`,
model `line-of-sight-v1`, `estimate: true`), computed from the same
installed raster, coordinates rounded to 0.01° first and never echoed back.
`sqm[i][j]` is the value at `altitudes[i]` and azimuth
`j × azimuthStepDegrees` (0 = north, 90 = east); `zenithSqm` equals the
zenith estimate of `GET /v1/sky-quality` at the rounded position.
`rasterChecksum` names the raster it was computed from, so a client keeps
the grid per place and per checksum. The grid is derived data: it is never
synchronized and never written into a plan. Errors, access and caching
(`private, max-age=86400`) are those of `GET /v1/sky-quality`, including
`503` `sky_quality_unavailable` with `Retry-After` when no raster is
installed. Shape: [`sky-quality-direction.schema.json`](sky-quality-direction.schema.json).

### Light-pollution map

`GET /v1/sky-quality/map/{z}/{x}/{y}.png` is held by `planner.mosaic`
(« Planification avancée »); a private server has no such route. It answers one 256 × 256
Web Mercator PNG tile (RGBA, zoom 3 to 10) of the estimated zenith sky
brightness of the region, cut on request from the same installed raster
as `GET /v1/sky-quality` (bilinear read, regional calibration) and coloured
with the ramp of the free horizon layer: transparent at 21.6 mag/arcsec²
and darker, strongest at 18.0 and brighter. Nothing is stored: a tile is
computed when asked. Access is a device bearer or the web session, then the
capability: `403` `capability_required` with `"capability":
"planner.mosaic"` without it. A tile outside the zoom range or the zoom's
grid is `400` `invalid_request`; without a raster, `503`
`sky_quality_unavailable` with `Retry-After`. The tile is cached
`private, max-age=86400`; its `ETag` names the renderer's version and the
raster's checksum, and a matching `If-None-Match` answers `304`. The value
at one point (« Bortle N · X mag/arcsec² · estimation ») is read from
`GET /v1/sky-quality`. Shape of the path, the headers and the error body:
[`sky-quality-map.schema.json`](sky-quality-map.schema.json).

`GET /v1/places/resolve` takes `latitude`, `longitude`, an optional
`elevation`, an optional `locale`, and an optional `reverse` flag (`"1"` to
also attempt reverse geocoding) — the same fields the web route's `resolve`
operation reads from its request body. It never exposes the web route's
free-text `search` operation. Invalid or out-of-range coordinates answer
`400` `invalid_request`. An elevation or reverse-geocode provider outage
degrades the result (`elevationSource: "unavailable"`, or the `name`/
`locality` fields omitted) rather than failing the request; the response is
`no-store`.

`GET /v1/places/search` takes a free-text `q` and an optional `locale` — the
same fields the web route's `search` operation reads from its request body,
and the one free-text operation `GET /v1/places/resolve` deliberately never
exposes. A trimmed `q` shorter than 2 or longer than 240 characters answers
`400` `invalid_request` before any provider call. On success it returns
`{ "results": [...] }`, each entry shaped like a `place-resolution.schema.json`
result plus `name` and `locality`, capped at 6 entries, throttled to roughly
one geocoder request per 1.1 seconds process-wide, and cached for 30 minutes
per query — the same throttle, cap and cache the web route's geocoder lookup
enforces, because both call the same function. A provider outage has no
degraded fallback to serve, so it answers `503` `place_search_unavailable`
rather than a partial result; the response is `no-store`.

### Suggested places

`GET /v1/places/suggested` takes `latitude`,
`longitude`, `radiusKm` (one of `10`, `30`, `60`, `100`), an optional
`types` (comma-separated among `viewpoint`, `campsite`, `park`, `picnic`;
absent = all four) and an optional `locale`. It answers named places from
OpenStreetMap, queried by the service at search time through an Overpass
instance (`ASTRO_PORTAL_PLACES_URL`), so the source never sees the observer.
The client sends the centre rounded to 0.05° (about 5 km) and the service
rounds it again before any query; the centre is never echoed back and the
service never computes a distance: the client measures the straight-line
distance from its own unrounded reference. A `park` is a national park or a
nature reserve, never an urban park.

The answer is `{ results, truncated, attribution, source: "osm", fetchedAt }`.
Each result carries `id` (`osm:node/123`), `name` (in `locale` when the place
has a name in that language), `type`, `latitude`, `longitude`, the optional
`website` (http or https only), `description` (the source's own sentence,
kept as written and never translated, up to 500 characters), `openingHours`,
`access`, `fee` and `elevation` (metres) the source holds, and `skyQuality`
(`{ bortle, sqm, estimate: true }`, the estimate of `GET /v1/sky-quality` at
the place from the installed raster, or `null` without one). At most 50
places, darkest first; `truncated` is `true` when more were found. Every
screen that shows a suggestion or its details shows `attribution` with its
link (the data is under the ODbL). Answers of the source are kept in memory
for 30 minutes (250 entries), behind the address search's process-wide
throttle (one source request per 1.1 seconds); nothing is stored. Errors:
`400` `invalid_request` before any source call; `503` `places_unavailable`
with `reason: "disabled"` when the service sets `ASTRO_PORTAL_PLACES=off`,
`reason: "source"` when the source refuses, fails or times out (10 s). The
response is `no-store`. Shape:
[`places-suggested.schema.json`](places-suggested.schema.json).

`GET /v1/places/known?source=osm&id=node/123` answers the current details of
one known place — the place a personal place is linked to by the planner
document's `knownPlace` (`{ source: "osm", id, type, linkedAt }`) — as
`{ place, attribution, source, fetchedAt }`, `place` in the shape of a
suggestion. The details are read when shown and never written into a plan.
`404` `known_place_gone` when the source no longer has it as a named place
of a supported type; the personal place is untouched. Access, throttle,
cache and `503` are those of `GET /v1/places/suggested`. Shape:
[`place-known.schema.json`](place-known.schema.json).

### Measured sky background

`POST /v1/place-sky-measures` receives the sky background
Astro Sync measured on the observer's own solved frames, on their computer
(`astro-sync sky`, the « Fond de ciel mesuré » section of its Analysis
screen; method `astro-sync-sky/1`): **one number per night**, in
mag/arcsec² — the median of the poses kept — with `poseCount` (how many poses
it is the median of), `methodVersion`, `observedAt` (the median instant), an
optional `sessionId` and an optional `site` (`{ latitude, longitude }` of
the frames), at least one of the two. Never an image, never a pose's own
value: the request is closed (`additionalProperties: false`) and any other
field is `400 invalid_request`. Free: no capability is read, on every
server — unlike `POST /v1/sessions/{id}/analyses`,
which needs `analysis.logs`.

The service decides the place and the night. A session with a plan gives the
plan's night (its date) and its place: the account's place whose id is the
plan location's `id`, else the account's place within 500 m of the plan
location. Otherwise (a direct session, no session, a plan whose place the
account does not know) the place is the account's place within 500 m of
`site` — the « déjà dans vos lieux » rule — and the night is the
date of `observedAt + round(longitude / 15) h − 12 h` at that place's
longitude, the rule of a place without a time zone. No place within 500 m:
`422 place_not_found`, nothing stored. `404 session_not_found` for a session
of another account or none. The site is used to find the place and never
kept.

One value per account, place and night (table `place_sky_measures`): a later
upload replaces it and answers `200` with `replaced: true`; a new one answers
`201` with `replaced: false`. The answer is `{ measure, replaced }`, `measure`
being `{ placeId, night, sqm, poseCount, methodVersion, observedAt, sessionId,
updatedAt }` (`placeId` is the place's id among the account's places, `night`
`YYYY-MM-DD`, `sqm` rounded to two decimals). A recorded value signals the
`places` change domain.

`GET /v1/place-sky-measures[?placeId=…]` answers
`{ places: [{ placeId, measures: [...] }] }`, each `measures` newest night
first, in the shape of `measure` without `placeId`. Either credential;
`no-store`. Shape:
[`place-sky-measure.schema.json`](place-sky-measure.schema.json).

How a value is shown is the clients', by one rule
written in `portal/sky-brightness-source.js` (`skyBrightnessSource`),
fixtured in `fixtures/sky-brightness-source.json`:
the latest measured night of the place, else the value the observer typed
in the place's form (the place document's additive `skyEntered`,
`{ sqm, enteredAt }`, `sqm` from 14 to 22.5 kept to the hundredth,
`enteredAt` an RFC 3339 instant whose fraction of a second has 1 to 9 digits,
as a phone writes microseconds; normalised by `normalizeSkyEntered`), else the model's estimate of `GET /v1/sky-quality`.
The two values not chosen are kept for the information sheet. The
applications keep the last answer of this route for offline reading.

### Data export

`GET /v1/data-export/summary[?date=AAAA-MM-JJ]` and
`GET /v1/data-export?parts=plans,places,equipment,framings,journal,sky-measurements&date=AAAA-MM-JJ`
(« Exporter mes données ») read the space of the
credential — a device bearer or a web session, never another space — on
every composition. Free:
no capability is asked for.

The summary (`data-export.schema.json` `$defs.summary`) gives, for each of
the six boxes, its `count` (`plans` adds `multiNight`, the multi-night
plans among them; `places` adds `horizons`, the recorded horizons) and
`estimatedBytes`, what its entry adds to the ZIP once deflated;
`overheadBytes` is what every archive carries (the README and the end
record). The file route answers the ZIP itself, `application/zip`,
`Content-Disposition: attachment; filename="planastro-export-<date>.zip"`,
holding a `README.md` whose first lines name `formatVersion: 1`, the
instant and the boxes present (`export/data-export-readme.schema.json`),
then one JSON per ticked box (`export/data-export-<box>.schema.json`), in
the order of the boxes. `plans.json` holds the plans, the planner's nights
they name by `nightId` (`nights`, each as the planner document keeps it)
and the multi-night plans. Without `parts`, the six boxes; an unknown box or
an empty list is `400` `invalid_request`. `date` is the browser's local
day; without it, the service's UTC day. Identifiers are kept as they are.
The gallery's images and the capture records stay in the space.

### Data import

`POST /v1/data-import/preview` and `POST /v1/data-import/confirm` (« Importer mes données ») take as their body the
`planastro-export-<date>.zip` of [Data export](#data-export) itself, read
under the space's `storage.max_file_bytes` (`413` `payload_too_large`
above it), and write only the space of the credential — a web session with
its CSRF token, or a device. Free. The service reads a file and its inflated
content up to caps of its own: above either, `413` `payload_too_large` with
`reason` (`data_import_max_bytes`, `data_import_max_content_bytes`; the
space's own `storage_max_file_bytes` otherwise) and, for the file,
`limitBytes`. The import is a write: in the read-only window of an ended
subscription the confirm answers `403` `entitlement_read_only` (the preview
writes nothing and stays open).

The file is read first by its `README.md`: `formatVersion: 1` only, any
other is `422` `unsupported_format_version` with `formatVersion`. Each JSON
present is held to its schema (`export/data-export-<box>.schema.json`); a
damaged entry (its CRC-32 or size), a missing README or a JSON that breaks
its schema is `400` `not_an_export` (with `part`, or `entry`). A file the
format does not name is ignored and listed in `notes.unknownFiles`.

Both routes answer the plan of the import (`data-import.schema.json`
`$defs.preview`): for each of the six boxes, `present` (false: « absent du
fichier »), `toImport` and `alreadyThere`; `kinds` splits the plans' box
into its `plans`, the planner's `nights` and the multi-night `projects`,
each with the same two counts; `notes` counts the records skipped —
`missingReference` (they name a plan or a framing neither the file nor the
space holds), `unreadable` (this portal cannot store them) — and the
records re-identified. A record whose identifier ANOTHER space of this
portal holds (an export of one space imported into another of the same
portal) is not skipped: it enters with a new identifier derived from the
importing space and the old one, every reference to it in the other records
(a plan item's framing and multi-night plan, a night's plan, a debriefing's
plan, a measure's night) is rewritten, and the report says it first:
`reidentified` and, by kind, `reidentifiedKinds` (`plans`, `projects`,
`framings`, `equipment`, `sessions`), all counted in `toImport`. The new
identifier is the same each time, so a second import of the file finds
the records and counts them « déjà présent »; the identifiers of a file
that collide nowhere (a private portal) are never touched. The confirm's
`written` also splits the plans' box (`written.kinds`). The space's offer is applied
to what enters, never by dropping it: a pending plan whose night already
holds a plan on an offer without `planner.multiple` (`archivedForNight`),
a pending plan beyond the numeric guard `sync.plan_limit`
(`archivedForPlanLimit`), a multi-night plan or a mosaic framing on an
offer without `planner.mosaic` (`archivedForCapability`) are imported
ARCHIVED — still counted in `toImport` — and the plans that were run enter
as they are, taking their night first. A private server's profile has no
such limit. The captures (images and files) are not part of the export and
are not imported. A record is
already there when its identifier exists in the space (a place: its id in
the planner document; a night of the planner: its id in the planner
document, counted in « Plans »; a night executed: its id, or its plan's
night; a measured sky background: its place and night); it is never
overwritten nor merged. The planner's nights enter its document after the
ones it holds (a space whose planner was never saved gets one). The
preview writes nothing. The confirm runs the same plan in one transaction —
everything to import enters, or nothing — and adds `written`; a second
import of the same file writes nothing. References between records are
kept as the file writes them; a debriefing enters with its plan, its
`decidedAt` kept as the file gives it (`null` from the phone).

### The project's calibration set

`/v1/space/calibration` is the observer's consent to the
project's own sky-brightness calibration set
([`docs/CONTRIBUTION_RULES.md`](https://github.com/sqound/astro-portal/blob/main/docs/CONTRIBUTION_RULES.md)):
off by default, asked only in the section of the space — a private server answers `404` `not_available` and never contributes.

- `GET` answers `{ consent: { enabled, consentedAt, kept, lastKeptAt } }`:
  whether it is on and since when (`null` when off), how many records the
  space has in the set, and when the last was kept or replaced.
- `PUT { "enabled": true|false }` (nothing else: `400` `invalid_request`)
  switches it and answers the same body. On, the values the space already
  holds are kept at once: each night measured at one of its places
  (`place_sky_measures`) and each place's entered value (`skyEntered`, on the
  planner's places or, for a place known only through the plans, the latest
  entry of a plan's place). Off, nothing new is kept; what is kept stays.
- `DELETE` — « Retirer mes contributions » — deletes the space's records and
  their ledger and switches the consent off: `{ withdrawn, consent }`. A
  release of the set already made cannot be recalled. Deleting the space does
  the same ([Deleting a space](#deleting-a-space)).

While the consent is on, a value stored or changed by `POST
/v1/place-sky-measures`, `PUT /v1/plans/{id}` or the planner's own write
keeps or replaces its record; a value removed (or whose place is deleted)
leaves the set. Each record is the seven fields of `$defs/record` — `kind`
(`measured`, `entered`), `sqm`, `band` (`camera`, `sqm`), `radianceLevel`
(the sky-quality raster's own 0–255 level of the place's cell), `region`
(`global` outside every calibration region), `year`, `methodVersion`
(measured only) — and nothing else: never a place, a coordinate, a name, a
path or an identifier. Without the installed raster no record is built.
Shapes, the record and the release file:
[`calibration-consent.schema.json`](calibration-consent.schema.json).

## Observation sessions and debriefing

A **plan** is an intention; an **observation session** is what happened. In
this document and in every schema it points at, "session" alone always means an
observation session; the browser's cookie-authenticated session is always
called a **web session**, as the authentication table above already does. The
distinction is not cosmetic: the portal has carried an unrelated `sessions`
table for web authentication since its first migration, and the observation
record is a separate table under its own name.

An observation session is an append-only journal: events are added by
identifier and never updated or deleted, which is why two devices annotating
the same night offline can be merged by a union with no conflict possible.
There is no event update or delete endpoint anywhere in this contract, by
design.

The machine-readable shapes are [`session.schema.json`](session.schema.json),
[`session-event-ingest.schema.json`](session-event-ingest.schema.json) and
[`debrief.schema.json`](debrief.schema.json). The body of each event type is
owned by the event-type registry — `schemas/session-events.md` and one
kebab-case file per snake_case type under `schemas/session-events/` — and is
reached through `session-event-ingest.schema.json`'s
`$defs.eventPayloadByType`, which is the one place this contract couples to it.
That registry also owns the submitted envelope
(`session-events/event-envelope.schema.json`); this contract restates it and
pins both, so any drift between the two fails loudly.

### Starting an observation session

`POST /v1/sessions` takes `{ planId?, startedAt, clientSessionId?, executionId?,
piloting?, origin? }` and
returns `{ session, created }`. `planId` is the server plan UUID, never the
browser client id carried inside a plan payload. A session with no `planId` is
a **direct session** and is a first-class path, not a degraded mode: many real
nights are improvised.

At most **one observation session exists per plan**. A second
`POST /v1/sessions` naming a `planId` that already has one answers `200` with
that session and `created: false` rather than creating a second. This is the
load-bearing half of the "no duplicate sessions" rule: two
surfaces, or one surface retrying after a timeout, converge on one journal. A
direct session has no such natural key, so a client that needs the same
guarantee sends its own `clientSessionId` (a ULID it generates once and reuses
on every retry); replay then returns the existing session. Without one, every
call creates a session, which is correct — two improvised nights are two
sessions.

Creating a session for a plan that is already frozen is allowed and returns
`201`. This is intended: a night that "took
another turn" produces a session from scratch even though the plan was marked
abandoned.

**Several plans in one execution** (additive). A night may be executed with several plans at once, each on its own
equipment. The rule above does not change: each plan still has its one
observation session. The request may carry two more fields, written when the
session is created and never changed by a replay that returns an existing
one:

- `executionId` — a ULID the client generates once for the execution; every
  session launched together for the plans of that execution carries the same
  one. An execution of a single plan sends none, and its session reads
  exactly as before.
- `piloting` — how the plan is piloted: `mount` (the application points a
  mount), `smart_telescope` (the plan runs in a smart telescope), `assisted`
  (manual pointing assisted by a phone's plate solving, possibly with a
  satellite phone) or `free` (the person drives the equipment, the plan is a
  reference).

A malformed `executionId` or an unknown `piloting` answers `400`
`invalid_request` with nothing written. The session resource carries both
fields only when they were given; a reader treats an absent field as `null`.

**The night of a capture** (additive: « a capture always has its
session »). When a capture is added and no session of its night exists, the
client builds one with the calls that already exist: `POST /v1/sessions`
`{ startedAt, clientSessionId, origin: "capture" }`, then `POST
/v1/sessions/{id}/finish` `{ finishedAt }`, then `PATCH /v1/sessions/{id}/night`
`{ place, targetIds }` (the place read from the photo or chosen, **without a
time zone**; `targetIds` may be empty: a session without a target is allowed,
the target being specified later on the session's page). The session has no
plan, so it is **not** a plan and does not count against the free account's
« one plan per night » limit; the capture itself counts against the free
account's number of captures (`capture_limit_reached` on the upload, which the
client asks `POST /v1/uploads/check` about before creating anything). A
session of `origin: "capture"` is editable like any other session (it is
neither read-only nor an import) and is listed by the Journal as `no_plan`.

### Changing the piloting of a session

The person may change the mode of a night while it runs (« Changer de mode » in
« Nuit en cours »: free, assisted, a mount, a smart telescope), so the
piloting written at the creation is no longer final. `PATCH /v1/sessions/{id}`
takes `{ piloting }` — one of `mount`, `smart_telescope`, `assisted` or `free`,
nothing else — and returns `200` the session resource with the new value.

- The same value again changes nothing: no `updatedAt` bump, no change
  published. A new value moves `updatedAt`, so that a device that reads
  `GET /v1/sessions?since=` learns it, and publishes the change to the other
  clients of the space.
- It also fills a session that was created without a piloting.
- A missing body, a body that is not JSON, a missing or unknown `piloting`
  answers `400` `invalid_request` with nothing written. A deleted session, an
  unknown one or another space's is `404` `session_not_found`; a night of the
  operator's catalogue is `409` `session_read_only`.
- A session already finished or debriefed accepts it too: the field says how
  the night was piloted, and the last value written wins. It is never held by a
  right.

### Appending events

`POST /v1/sessions/{id}/events` takes `{ events: [...] }`, at most 500 per
batch. Each submitted event carries `eventId`, `type`, `recordedAt` and
`payload` — and nothing else. `originDeviceId` is **derived from the
authenticated credential**, never accepted from a client, on the same principle
that already forbids a client-supplied tenant id.

Ingestion is an idempotent union keyed on `(sessionId, eventId)`. An existing
pair is skipped: the stored event is left exactly as it is, **whatever the
resubmitted payload says**, because an event is never edited. The response
accounts for every submitted event in exactly one of three lists, so nothing is
silently dropped:

```json
{
  "sessionId": "4d2b7e10-8a6c-4f53-9b1d-7e0a3c5f2b84",
  "receivedAt": "2026-09-16T09:12:00.000Z",
  "accepted": ["01J8Z9QK7R4M2N6P8S0T1V3W5X"],
  "duplicate": ["01J8Z9QK7R4M2N6P8S0T1V3W5Y"],
  "rejected": [
    {
      "eventId": "01J8Z9QK7R4M2N6P8S0T1V3W60",
      "code": "unknown_event_type",
      "message": "This event type is not in the registry."
    }
  ],
  "counts": { "accepted": 1, "duplicate": 1, "rejected": 1 },
  "clockSkewMs": -1200
}
```

**A faulty event is rejected alone; the batch is not.** An event whose `type`
is outside the registry, or whose payload fails its registry schema, is
reported in `rejected` with its reason while every other event of the batch is
stored; the call itself is `200`. The doctrine decides this: the batch is an
arbitrary transport grouping of an append-only journal, so refusing 499 good
observations because the five-hundredth came from a newer application version
would lose field truth for a packaging accident — and worse, the client would
resend the same poisoned batch forever, never converging. Rejecting the single
faulty event lets the client record the reason, drop it, and move on. The
alternative considered was refusing the whole batch with `400`, which is
simpler to implement and easier to reason about transactionally; it was
rejected for that unbounded-retry failure mode.

The batch **is** refused whole, with nothing written, for envelope-level faults
only — malformed JSON, a missing or empty `events` array, more than 500 entries
(`event_batch_too_large`) — because those are facts about the request, not
about the night. The accepted subset of a batch is written in one transaction:
a partial write is never visible.

Events are accepted by a session in any state, `finished` included. A finished
session still takes appends, because finishing marks the end of the field
night, not the end of the journal: a second device reconnecting days later, and
Astro Sync attaching analyses, both write to sessions that are long
closed. The alternative — refusing with `409` and forcing the late batch
through the supplement path — was rejected because it would either lose the
data or duplicate a mechanism for the simplest case.

`eventId` is a 26-character Crockford base32 ULID, the registry envelope's own
rule. It is an **identity, not an order**: a client orders events by
`recordedAt`, then `receivedAt`, then `eventId`. Events generated by the server
(see the mapping below) use deterministic ULIDs: the random tail is replaced by
a hash, and the time part by a night anchor plus an emission ordinal, so that
nothing in the generating request can change an identifier. They are therefore
ordered but not time-generated, and `eventId` remains the last tiebreak rather
than a timestamp to read.

A **stored** event additionally carries `timing`, an additive, server-set field
saying what kind of claim its `recordedAt` makes: `observed` when the instant
was measured — a device's clock for a field event, the server's for a note
actually written at debriefing time; `planned` when it was read off the plan's
own time slot; `reconstructed` when it is a session-level fallback applied to
an act that has no time of its own. An interface must not present a `planned`
or `reconstructed` instant as a measured time. It is derived exactly as
`originDeviceId` is, so the submitted envelope — the registry's, not this
contract's — is untouched and a device keeps sending what it already sent; a
client that tries to set it is refused. A portal predating the field omits it,
and a reader then falls back to the only signal it already had, a null
`originDeviceId`.

It carries too `source` (additive, server-set, never accepted from a client,
derived like `originDeviceId` from the device's token): `astro-sync` when the
device that sent the event is a device of the application Astro Sync (its
detections), `declared` for anything else. Only `astro-sync` lets an interface
write « Détecté par Astro Sync »; a portal predating the field omits it and the
interface says nothing of the origin. Four types are added to the registry
(`exposure_change`, `guiding_timeouts`, `sensor_temperature_drift`,
`guiding_spikes`); a reader tolerates a type it does not know.
Fifteen more are added, all optional and additive (`mount_connected`,
`mount_goto`, `mount_stop`, `mount_link_lost`, `mount_link_back`,
`mount_disconnected`, `mount_parked`,
`mount_aligned`, `pointing_help`, `device_connected`, `device_goto`, `device_stop`,
`device_parked`, `device_link_lost`, `device_link_back`): the events of the night's
mount or smart telescope, kept in the session (`schemas/session-events.md`).
One more is added, optional and additive (`plan_edited`: a target added, removed or
moved while the night ran, with the slots the change gave them): a plan changed during
the night is an event of the session, read by the debriefing and the journal
(`schemas/session-events.md`).
One more is added, optional and additive (`device_instrument`, contract revision
2026-11-03: a sample of what a smart telescope says of itself — filter, hygrometer,
dew heater, battery, temperatures, frames kept and rejected, pointing given up —
written every 5 minutes and at each change, private in a shared night); a reader that
predates it ignores it (`schemas/session-events.md`, « The instrument kind »).
One more, optional, additive and versioned (`guided_evening`: a guided evening that
ran under the session's night ended — its route, the way of observing, the steps seen,
its duration): written once at the evening's end, shown on the session's page
(`schemas/session-events.md`).

### Finishing an observation session

`POST /v1/sessions/{id}/finish` takes an optional `finishedAt` and returns the
session. It is idempotent: a second call returns the already finished session
and never moves a `finishedAt` that was already recorded. Finishing emits no
event of its own — it is a session state, and `pause`/`resume` are the event
types that describe interruptions during the night.

It also takes an optional `closedBy` (additive):
`automatic` when the phone closed the night by itself at its end, `manual` when
the person pressed « Terminer ». The service keeps it as sent and renders it as
such on the session resource (`closedBy`); a session that never said — an
earlier one, a debriefing's, an import's — carries no `closedBy` and a reader
treats it as unknown. A replay of the finish never moves it, a reopen clears it
(the night is running again), and any other value answers `400`
`invalid_request` with nothing written.

### Reopening an observation session

`POST /v1/sessions/{id}/reopen` (« Reprendre la
nuit ») takes an optional `reopenedAt` and returns the session, open again:
`finishedAt` is cleared and `status` is `open`. A night stopped because it
clouded over, then resumed when it cleared, is the case: the device decides
whether the time between the stop and the resume was a pause and writes it to
the journal as ordinary `pause`/`resume` events (the journal stays append-only;
reopening emits no event of its own).

- A session that is not finished is returned unchanged (idempotent).
- A session whose plan is frozen — debriefed or cancelled — is refused with
  `409 session_debriefed`: its night is closed for good.
- The latest gesture wins, whatever the order of arrival: a `reopen` whose
  `reopenedAt` is older than the recorded `finishedAt` (a later stop) changes
  nothing, and a `finish` whose `finishedAt` is older than the recorded
  `reopenedAt` changes nothing either. A device that went offline replays its
  reopen first, then its events, then its next finish; a second device that
  still believes the session finished converges on the same state.

### The night boundaries of a plan

Everything below — the queue, the "night has passed" test, the unsynchronized
device warning, and every per-target timestamp of the mapping — rests on two
server-derived instants on the plan resource: `nightStartAt` and `nightEndAt`.
They are additive, read-only, and nullable together.

**The definition is the planner's own, and that is a correctness requirement,
not a preference.** A plan item's `startMinute` and `endMinute` are whole
minutes counted from the start of the night the step-4 timeline was drawn
against (`availabilityWindow`/`nightMinutes`, measuring from `night.start`). If the
server derived a different night, every slot would silently land at the wrong
instant. So the server computes, for the plan's night-of `date` and its
`payload.location`:

- an observer at the place's latitude, longitude and elevation;
- local civil **noon** of `date` in the place's IANA `timeZone` — an absent or
  unusable zone is read as `UTC`, exactly as the planner's `validTimeZone`
  does — as the search anchor, which is what selects the following evening
  rather than the preceding dawn;
- `nightStartAt` = the first **descending** crossing of solar altitude −18°
  after that noon (astronomical dusk);
- `nightEndAt` = the first **ascending** crossing of −18° after
  `nightStartAt` (astronomical dawn).

Astronomical night, −18°: nautical and civil twilight are not used anywhere in
the planner and are not used here. Both values are truncated to whole seconds,
so a patch-level change in the ephemeris library cannot move a stored boundary
by a millisecond and with it an event identifier.

**A plan's `darkness` and `nightSpan`** are two optional,
additive properties of the payload ([`plan.schema.json`](plan.schema.json)):
the darkness threshold of the plan's night (`astronomical`, `nautical`,
`civil`; absent = `astronomical`) and the span of the night it keeps
(`{ "mode": "astronomical" }` or `{ "mode": "custom", "start", "end" }`;
absent = `astronomical`). The server stores both **untouched**, whatever their
value, through `PUT /api/plans` and `PUT /v1/plans/{planId}`, and derives
nothing from them: `nightStartAt` and `nightEndAt` stay the −18° boundaries
above. A reader normalises them once (`packages/portal/src/planner/state.js`,
`normalizePlanDarkness` and `normalizeNightSpan`) and reads the plan's own
night through `planNightWindow` (`packages/planner/src/planner.js`).

**Both are `null`, together, whenever the payload does not permit the
calculation**: no `location`, a latitude or longitude that is absent,
non-numeric or out of range, the legacy `0,0` null-island placeholder the
portal already excludes (`packages/portal/src/persistence/planner-state-repository.js`,
`knownPlannerLocation`), or a night at which the Sun never crosses −18° at all
— polar summer and polar winter. The planner substitutes noon and the
following noon for its own geometry in that last case; **the server must not**:
a "night" running from noon to noon is not a night, and a plan admitted to the
queue on that basis would be asserting a night that never happened. The
conservative rule of this contract is unchanged and now has a precise scope:
a portal that cannot compute a night end does not invent one, and a plan with
a null `nightEndAt` never enters the queue.

**When they are computed.** On every plan write that produces a new revision —
a creating or updating `PUT /v1/plans/{planId}`, a version restore, and the
restore a `set_aside` debriefing performs — from the payload that becomes
current. They are a projection of the payload, never a client input: a `PUT`
body that happens to contain fields of these names does not set them, exactly
as `tenantId` is never accepted from a client.

**Plans already stored.** Every plan an earlier portal wrote carries
`night_end_at = NULL`; the column exists and is indexed, but no code path ever
populated it (`planner-state-repository.js` writes the literal `null`). Those
plans are caught up **lazily and durably**: when
`GET /v1/plans/debrief-queue` scans the tenant's `active`, non-frozen plans, it
derives and persists the boundaries of any whose `nightStartAt` is still null,
inside its own transaction, before applying the queue test. The cost is bounded
by one tenant's active plan count, the derivation is a pure function of
`(date, latitude, longitude, timeZone)` and is memoized, and a plan whose place
is unusable is marked as resolved-to-null so it is not retried on every read. A
frozen plan is never recomputed: its boundaries are history.

`POST /v1/plans/{planId}/debrief` performs the same catch-up, on its one plan,
inside its own transaction and **before** anything else — before the
`plan_not_debriefable` test, before the session, before a single event. Two
reasons: a surface may reach the route without ever having read the queue, and
the derived `nightStartAt` is what anchors every generated identifier, so it
has to be a stored value both racing transactions read rather than one a
transaction might compute for itself. Once the plan is frozen it is fixed, so a
later queue read can never move it.

This is preferred to a one-shot data migration, which would have to run
astronomical code inside `migrations.ts` — where nothing else does — and would
still not cover a plan written afterwards by a client predating this rule. A
migration remains available as an optional warm-up and would change no
behaviour. Nothing else in the portal reads these columns, so the catch-up is
observable only as a queue that stops being empty.

**A plan's `launchedSchedule`** is another optional,
additive property of the payload ([`plan.schema.json`](plan.schema.json)): the
plan as it stood when its night was launched,
`[{ "planItemId", "targetId", "startMinute", "endMinute" }]`, one entry per
slotted item in the order of the slots. The mobile application writes it once,
at the launch, before any start is trimmed; nothing rewrites it afterwards. The
server stores it **untouched** through `PUT /api/plans` and
`PUT /v1/plans/{planId}`, the web planner keeps it through its saves, and
nothing is derived from it on the server. The execution screen draws the
night's drifts against it, and a plan without it (launched before it existed,
or not launched yet) simply has no frozen reference. Each entry may also carry
the lines frozen at the launch, `acquisition` and `ruleVersion` ([What to shoot](#what-to-shoot-the-acquisition-lines-of-a-plan)); the service
checks and normalises those two fields alone.

### The debrief queue

`GET /v1/plans/debrief-queue` returns the nights waiting for an answer. The
internal plan of a guided evening (`payload.guidedEvening: true`) never
qualifies. Any other plan qualifies when its `status` is `active`, it is not
frozen, its `nightEndAt` is
non-null, and either that `nightEndAt` is **strictly before** the server instant
or its execution session is already **finished** (a night closed by hand at
3 a.m. is over for the person, whatever the derived end says; the staleness of
a device is then measured against the moment the session finished). The queue
is never truncated — chaining three new-moon nights and debriefing on Sunday is
the expected case — and reading it never gates anything.

A plan closed as abandoned (`closedAs`, see
[A finished night is carried by the plan](#a-finished-night-is-carried-by-the-plan-closedas))
is decided « Abandonné » by that very write and is not `active` any more: it is
not in the queue.

A plan whose night the server could not derive is therefore silent, for ever,
and that is deliberate: the queue's whole claim is "this night has passed", and
it is not made about a night nothing is known about. The interface that wants
to debrief such a plan anyway reaches
`POST /v1/plans/{planId}/debrief` directly, which accepts it — see
[Debriefing before the night ends](#the-debriefing-decision) below.

Each entry carries:

- `plan` — the current plan resource, `nightStartAt` and `nightEndAt`
  included;
- `nightStartAt` — repeated on the entry beside the `nightEndAt` the queue
  already carried, because it is the origin the plan items' slots are counted
  from and an interface showing "what you planned for that night" needs it to
  place them;
- `staleDevices` — every unrevoked device token of the tenant that can hold
  offline plan changes (the phone app; never Astro Sync, which keeps no plan
  data offline) whose `lastSyncAt` is null or earlier than `nightEndAt`, with
  its `label` and that timestamp, so the interface can name the device and the
  exact time. It is a
  warning and never a refusal: the phone may have been in airplane mode,
  broken, or used only for reading. It depends on the derived `nightEndAt` and
  is therefore exactly as available as the queue itself: a plan whose night the
  server could not derive raises no warning because it raises no entry, and no
  surface is told a device is stale relative to a night nobody computed;
- `versions` — **present only when a set-aside version exists**, and then
  holding exactly two readings of the plan, **field first**. Each carries the
  literal `base` value (`"current"` or `"set_aside"`) to send back in the
  decision, an `origin` of `field` or `web`, its `createdAt` and its `payload`.
  `origin` is `field` when the version was written by a paired device
  (`plan_versions.origin_device_id` is set) and `web` otherwise. There are
  never three: there is no line-by-line merge assistant, and no third copy
  accumulates;
- `openSession` — non-null while a session for this plan is `open` or `paused`.
  **A surface must not present a debriefing or a version-resolution prompt for
  such an entry**, and must not present one while it holds a local open or
  paused session for that plan that the server cannot see. This is the field
  rule "no resolution is ever requested at 2 a.m." expressed where it has an
  API consequence; the purely local half stays a client obligation.

### The debriefing decision

`POST /v1/plans/{planId}/debrief` carries
`{ outcome, baseVersion?, setAsideVersionId?, session?, sessionAdjustments?, note? }`.
A well-formed decision always answers `200` — a first one and a replay alike;
the refusals are listed at the end of this subsection and in
[Sessions and debriefing error codes](#sessions-and-debriefing-error-codes).

- `executed` — the session is generated from the plan payload; the plan becomes
  `executed` and is frozen;
- `partial` — the same, with `sessionAdjustments` deciding per target what was
  actually done; the plan becomes `partial` and is frozen;
- `abandoned` — the plan becomes `abandoned` and is frozen, and ([The debriefing makes the session](#the-debriefing-makes-the-session))
  a finished session of outcome `abandoned` is created or kept with its
  `reason`, still with no event. Unlike the other two outcomes, `abandoned` is accepted at **any**
  time — before the night, during it, or after — because "the clouds have
  already won" is knowable well before `nightEndAt`; see
  [Debriefing before the night ends](#the-debriefing-decision) for the one
  guard this admits. An existing session is never removed by this outcome: if
  one is still open or paused it is finished, and a session that already
  holds field events is refused rather than silently declared abandoned — the
  same section.

Freezing sets `frozenAt` and makes the plan read-only. The intention and the
fact then coexist untouched, which is the pair the future "planned versus
achieved" analyses need.

**Per-target outcome and acquired time** (additive). Under
`partial`, a target adjustment may also say `partial` — the target was
observed for part of its slot — and `done`, `modified` and `partial` may carry
`acquiredMinutes`, the observing time the person declares for that target in
whole minutes (`≥ 0`; ignored under `skipped`). The adjustments the decision
read are stored with it, normalized (`reason` only under `skipped`,
`acquiredMinutes` only on an observed target, notes never — they are journal
events), and read back as `debrief.schema.json`'s `record`: in
`GET /v1/sessions/{id}`'s `debrief` and on the web debriefing page. An
`executed` or `abandoned` decision stores an empty list. A declared
`acquiredMinutes` is what the night's summary counts for that target instead
of computing it from the journal (`packages/portal/src/debrief-summary.js`).

**Idempotency.** A second call for a plan that already carries `executed`,
`partial` or `abandoned` returns `200` with the existing plan and session,
`replayed: true`, `createdSession: false` and `eventsCreated: 0`. Nothing is
created, and the submitted body has no effect — including a different
`outcome`. This is the hard requirement "no double prompts, no duplicate
sessions": with the queue driven by server state, a night answered on the web
has already left the mobile queue, and a race between the two surfaces
converges instead of duplicating. Someone who wants to correct a debriefing
appends to the session; the plan is history and is not rewritten. A plan that
is `archived` without ever having been debriefed answers `409` `plan_frozen`:
there is no earlier decision to replay.

**The divergence resolution point.** When the queue offered two versions,
`baseVersion` is required and states which one describes the night;
`set_aside` additionally carries `setAsideVersionId`. Answering `set_aside`
performs the same operation as
`POST /v1/plans/{planId}/versions/{versionId}/restore` — the set-aside payload
becomes a new plan revision and the payload it replaces becomes a `history`
version — and only then freezes the plan and generates the session from it.
Answering `current` leaves the set-aside version exactly where it is: still
recoverable, purged only after the documented retention period. Either way
nothing is overwritten and nothing is deleted, and the person answered by
pointing at what they did rather than by resolving a technical conflict.
Omitting `baseVersion` when two versions exist is `400`
`set_aside_version_required` — silently picking one would be the automatic
resolution the doctrine forbids.

**Debriefing before the night ends.** For `executed` and `partial` only: a
plan whose `nightEndAt` is non-null and still in the future, **and whose
execution session is not finished**, answers `409`
`plan_not_debriefable`: claiming a night was executed, in whole or in part,
before it has happened is not a decision anyone means to make. A plan whose
`nightEndAt` is null is accepted, deliberately asymmetrically with the queue
rule above: the queue stays conservative and silent when it does not know the
night, while the route stays usable for a surface that does.

That asymmetry was written when `nightEndAt` was null on every plan and it
survives the night derivation unchanged, but its meaning narrows. It is no
longer the ordinary case — a plan with a place and a date now has its
boundaries — and covers exactly the plans the server legitimately knows nothing
about: no usable place, or a polar night with no −18° crossing. For those the
person in front of the screen knows whether the night happened and the server
does not, so the route trusts them; for everything else the `409` above holds.
A debriefing accepted this way produces a session whose target events are all
`reconstructed`, because with no `nightStartAt` no slot can be placed.

**`abandoned` has no such wait** (raised by the
mobile execution screen's own "this plan will not happen — cancelled"
control): the person may know at 9 p.m. that the sky has already closed for
the night, well before `nightEndAt`, and making them wait out a night that
will not happen serves nobody. `outcome: "abandoned"` is therefore accepted
regardless of `nightEndAt` — future, past, or null alike — with exactly one
guard: **if an observation session already exists for this plan and holds at
least one event**, `abandoned` is refused with `409` `plan_not_debriefable`
("the night has started; debrief it as `partial`") — a session with field
events is precisely the fact "abandoned" denies, and the doctrine's way to
correct a wrong statement is to append or to choose `partial`, never to force
a fiction into the frozen record. A session that exists but is still
**empty** — open, paused, or already finished, but with no event ever
appended to it — does not describe a night that happened either, so
`abandoned` is accepted and that empty session is finished (`status:
"finished"`, no `finishedAt` moved if one was already recorded) without
generating any event of its own: the journal stays append-only and nothing is
deleted, there is simply nothing yet to append to it.

### From a plan to an observation session: the initial-event mapping

This mapping is normative for `executed` and `partial`. An implementer and a
test must be able to follow it to the character.

**Inputs.** The plan `P` after any `set_aside` restore, its `payload` `B`, its
`revision` `R` (the revision that results from the restore, when one happened),
its stored `nightStartAt` and `nightEndAt`, the request `D`, and the server
instant `now`.

**The observation session.** If one already exists for `P`, it is reused and
`createdSession` is `false`; only the events it does not already hold are
appended. Otherwise a session is created with:

| Field        | Value                                                                       |
| ------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `id`         | a new server UUID                                                           |
| `planId`     | `P.id`                                                                      |
| `startedAt`  | `D.session.startedAt`, else `P.nightStartAt`, else `<B.date>T00:00:00.000Z` |
| `finishedAt` | `D.session.finishedAt`, else `P.nightEndAt`, else `startedAt`               |
| `status`     | `finished`                                                                  |

When a decision supplies BOTH `D.session.startedAt` and
`D.session.finishedAt`, the end must be strictly after the start: anything
else answers `400 invalid_request` and freezes nothing — a night that ends
before it starts is a mistyped hour on one surface or the other, never a fact
worth storing. A single supplied bound is not checked against the derived one.

The first draft fell back to `P.nightEndAt` for `startedAt` as well, for the
sole reason that no night start existed; with `nightStartAt` derived, the
session's reconstructed window is the night itself rather than a single instant
repeated twice.

Both fallbacks are a **reconstruction, not a measurement**: the server cannot
know when the person actually started. `<B.date>T00:00:00.000Z` is the night-of
anchor, a convention marker and not a claim about clock time; a surface that
knows the real window sends it in `D.session` and should, because it is the
only one that can. An interface never presents a reconstructed window as a
measured time — and every event derived from one says so in its own `timing`,
which is where that obligation becomes machine-readable instead of a sentence
in this document.

**The plan's time slots.** A plan item carries the step-4 timeline's interval:
`startMinute` and `endMinute`, and often `durationMinutes`, in whole minutes
counted from `nightStartAt`. For an item `I`:

- `slotStart(I)` is `I.startMinute` when it is a finite number ≥ 0; otherwise
  `I` **has no slot**;
- `slotEnd(I)` is `I.endMinute` when it is a finite number ≥ `slotStart(I)`;
  else `slotStart(I) + I.durationMinutes` when `durationMinutes` is finite and
  positive; else `slotStart(I)`.

A zero-length slot is a real value, not a missing one: a target added from the
catalogue on mobile lands as `{ startMinute: 0, endMinute: 0, durationMinutes:
0 }` until the schedule editor places it. Its two
target events then carry the same instant, which is the truthful reading of a
placeholder the person never positioned. The mapping does not widen it to an
invented duration.

**Chronological order.** Items are processed in slot order: sorted by
`slotStart`, then `slotEnd`, then payload index; every item with no slot comes
after every item with one, in payload index order. This order is a pure
function of `B`. It is the order the events are emitted in, and — through the
identifiers below — the order a reader recovers when two events share a
timestamp. The first draft used payload order and relied on `receivedAt` to
preserve it, which it cannot: a generated batch carries one `receivedAt` for
all of its events.

**Targets → events.** For each item `I` of `B.items`, in that chronological
order, let `A` be the entry of `D.sessionAdjustments.targets` whose `targetId`
equals `I.id`:
